みずほ経済 EXPRESS

みずほ GDP ナウ（2026 年 10 月中旬時点） 
～7～9 月期 GDP は前期比＋0.03％（年率＋0.10％）と推計～

調査部  シニア日本経済エコノミスト 越山祐資 
同             エコノミスト  阿部大樹 
080-1069-4835 yusuke.koshiyama@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 10 月中旬時点の「みずほ GDP ナウ」は 7～9 月期 GDP を前期比＋0.10％と予測 
みずほ総合研究所では景気動向をタイムリーに把握するべく、浦沢（2023）等を参考にGDPナウキ
ャスティングに取り組んできた。これを「みずほGDPナウ」と呼称することとし、太田他（2024）で
はデータドリブン予測である「みずほGDPナウ」が民間予測平均並みの予測精度を確保できることを
示した。その上で、酒井他（2024）をはじめとして、月次経済指標を用いたGDPナウキャスティング
の結果を紹介してきたところである。 
本稿では10月中旬時点までに得られる月次経済指標を用い、 「みずほGDPナウ」 による7～9月期GDP
の予測結果を紹介する。予測に用いたデータは、8月分の鉱工業生産、 消費活動指数、 所定外労働時間、
消費財出荷指数、第3次産業活動指数、9月分の中小企業景況調査（売上げ見通しDI）である1。図表1
には、予測に使用した各月次経済指標の推移を示している。 
図表 1 各月次経済指標の推移

（注）青字は改善、赤字は悪化を示す

日本銀行「消費活動指数」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

### Page 2

2

モデルによる10月中旬時点における7 ～9月期実質GDP成長率の予測値は、図表2のとおり前期比＋
0.03％ （年率＋0.10％）となった。 他機関による予測では、東京財団政策研究所による10月15日時点の
ナウキャスティングが同＋0.29％（年率＋1.18％）、日本経済研究センターが公表した10月のESPフォ
ーキャスト調査（回答期間：2026年9月30日～10月7日、回答者37名）における民間予測値平均が前期
比年率＋0.08％となっている。「みずほGDPナウ」の予測値は、民間コンセンサスよりやや高いもの
の、概ね近い伸びを予測していると言える。 
なお、多くの民間エコノミストが保守的な見方をしている理由の一つには、GDP統計作成上のテク
ニカルな要因がある。4～6月期には、 中東情勢悪化を背景に原油 ・ ナフサ輸入が大幅に減少したほか、
石油化学製品の供給途絶懸念から幅広い企業で在庫積み増しの動きが生じた。やや直感に反するが、
いずれもGDPに対しては押し上げ要因となる。 そのため、4～6月期GDPは前期比＋0.36％ （年率＋1.45％）
とプラス成長になったものの、 成長率が示すほど景気の実態は強くなかったとみるのが自然であろう。
反対に、7～9月期については、4～6月期の反動で輸入増・在庫減の動きが顕在化し、GDPには一定の
下方圧力がかかる可能性がある。みずほ総合研究所が9月に公表したレポート2でも、こうしたテクニ
カル要因を考慮の上、7～9月期GDPは横ばい圏の伸びにとどまると予想している。 
■ 「みずほ GDP ナウ」による予測値下方修正の主因は生産・消費関連指標の悪化 
「みずほGDPナウ」 による7～9月期のGDP（1次速報値） の予測にあたっては10月中旬までに公表さ
れた月次経済指標を使って予測値を4回更新した。図表3では、予測値の更新過程と各月次経済指標の
寄与度を示している3。予測値が下方修正された主因は、8月の鉱工業生産、 消費財出荷指数の低下だ。
特に鉱工業生産指数については、7月末に発生した熊本地震による影響が下押し要因になったとみら
れる。 大手自動車メーカーで工場の操業が停止した影響で自動車が前月比▲6.8％と大幅に減産したほ
か、 電子部品 ・ デバイスも同▲1.9％の減産となった。7月調査時点の生産予測指数は前月比＋3.1％（ 補
図表 2 各機関の実質GDP成長率予測値 図表 3 予測値の改定過程と各経済指標の寄与度

ト調査」等より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

寄与度を示す。「みずほGDPナウ」は各経済指標の結果を公表
前に予測しており （モデル予測値） 、 寄与度はモデル予測値と
実績値の乖離幅から計算される。 なお、 各経済指標の確報を受
けて指標が改定された場合も寄与度に反映される

### Page 3

3

正値ベース）と増産が見込まれていたが、大幅に下振れた格好だ。一方、世界的に旺盛なAI・データ
センター関連需要を背景に、半導体製造装置を含む生産用機械などが増産したものの、熊本地震の影
響を打ち返せなかった。消費財出荷指数の低下については、4～5月の耐久財特需が一巡したことで反
動減の動きが生じた。非耐久財の出荷は前月から増加したものの、7月の落ち込みも踏まえると、 基調
としては力強さを欠いていると言えよう。長雨や低温、といった天候不順により生鮮野菜価格が上昇
しているほか、食肉も値上がりが大きく、こうした手に取る機会が多い品目の価格上昇が家計の節約
志向を強め、個人消費の下押し圧力となっているとみられる。9月についても、食品のほか、原油・石
油化学製品等のコスト高の影響で幅広い品目の価格上昇が加速する見込みであり、個人消費は冴えな
い動きが続く可能性がある。 
以上のように、7～9月期成長率は低い伸びに抑えられる公算が大きい。前述のとおり原油・ナフサ
の輸入増、 企業の在庫調整といった動きはGDP統計上、成長率に対してマイナスに寄与する。加えて、
熊本地震による生産減や天候要因による消費支出抑制の動きが下押し要因になる見込みだ。 
次回の 「みずほGDPナウ」については、11月16日に公表される7～9月期GDP1次速報を挟んで、10～
12月期GDPのリアルタイム予測に取り組む。10月分の鉱工業生産や消費活動指数の結果等を踏まえて
推計し、12月中旬頃のレポート発刊を予定している。原油高等を受けた物価上昇圧力の強まりや堅調
なAI・データセンター関連需要など、プラス・マイナス双方の影響が交錯する中、ナウキャストによ
る景気動向の把握はより重要なものとなるだろう。

1 ステップワイズ法により使用データを採択した。詳細は太田ほか（2024）を参照されたい。今後のモデルの予測精度のパフォ
ーマンス評価等を踏まえ、採択するデータについては見直しを行う可能性がある。 
2 詳細については、服部直樹「年率＋1.4％と 1 次速報から小幅に上方修正（4～6 月期2 次QE）」（2026 年9 月8 日）を参照され
たい。 
3 太田他（2024）で示した枠組みと同様、図表 2 の折れ線が各時点における実質GDP 成長率の予測値であり、月次の経済指標が
新たに公表されたり改定されたりすることで予測値が更新される。棒グラフは、予測値の改定幅、すなわち前回予測との差を各
月次経済指標で寄与度分解したもので、寄与度を合計するとモデル予測値の改定幅と一致する。なお、 「みずほGDP ナウ」は各
月次経済指標についても公表前に予測値を計算しており、各指標の寄与度はモデル予測値と実績値の乖離から計算されることか
ら、寄与度の符号は経済指標の変化の方向とは必ずしも一致しない。

4Q26/2027 年獲利將續創新高，受惠 DDR4 供不應求延續 
預估 4Q26 營收季增 13.2%至 1,512.46 億 元 ， 其 中 預 期ASP 再 季 增
15~20%、雖收斂但仍維持雙位數季增；毛利率季增 0.4ppts 至 86.1%，報
價維持高檔下與 3Q26 相近；EPS 季增 11.9%至 28.42 元。展望後續，月產
能未來四年內將由目前約 65kwpm 分階段新增 35.9kwpm 的產能、2H27 開
始貢獻，供需緊俏延續下 ASP 將維持高檔，4Q26/2027 年營運續創新高。 
2027 年 DRAM 供需緊俏與報價上行持續，主要由 Server 驅動 
本中心預期 2027 年 DRAM 持續供不應求，報價仍在上行循環，最主要驅動
力 Server 於整體位元需求佔比過半且供需差達 20%以上(高於整體 DRAM
的約 10%)，其中預期 2027 年 HBM 市場規模年增 243%，Nvidia VR200 搭
載 SOCAMM2 位元需求成長達 6 倍，General Server 亦佔整體 Server 需求
一半比重，相關 RDIMM、LPDRAM 成長強勁；同時新增產能相對需求成長
仍有限，三大原廠 2027 年底 DRAM 投片月產能僅年增 10~15%，中國同業
長鑫存儲雖擴產積極仍僅佔整體產能約 15%，主要供應中國國內需求。此外，
DDR4 於消費性/網通/Server 仍具龐大需求，目前 AI Server 仍有約 10%週
邊應用搭載 DDR4，供給則在國際原廠 EOL 下大幅縮減，長鑫存儲重心亦
已轉往 DDR5，預估 2027 年 DDR4 仍有 20%以上的供需缺口(詳見圖 7)。 
產品組合持續往 Server/AI 提升，中長期營運展望正向 
綜 合 上 述 ， 預 估2026/27 年 營 收 年 增 525.5%/66.8% 至 4,165.31 億元
/6,946.17 億元 ，毛利率年增 60ppts/2ppts 至 82.5%/84.5% ，EPS 年增
3457.6%/68.6%至 75.93/128.01 元。考量 1) 公司現為 DDR4 第一大廠且同
時具備 DDR5 產品布局、2) 產品組合持續往 Server/AI 提升、3) WoW 客製
化產品有望帶來更穩定獲利支撐，中長期營運更加穩健，以 2.5 倍 PB(過去
三年落在 0.4~2.8 倍)、2027 年 BVPS 2 30.7 元，給予買進、目標價 570 元。 
元大觀點 
⧫  
4Q26 預期 ASP 再季增 15~20%，2027 年 DDR4 供不應求延續下合約
價仍維持高檔，獲利將年增 68.6%續創新高。 
⧫  
2027 年 DRAM 產業 供 需 緊 俏 及 報 價 上 行 持 續 ， 最 主 要 驅 動 力 為
Server 相關 DRAM 訂單需求大幅成長，新增產能則相對有限。 
⧫  
產品組合續往 Server/AI 提升，中長期營運更加穩健，給予買進、目標
價 570 元(2.5x 2027 年 BVPS)。

### Page 2

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：記憶體 2026 年 10 月 27 日 Nanya Tech 第2 頁，共 20頁
營運分析
3Q26 獲利季增 74.6%，主因 ASP 季增>60%
3Q26 營收 1,336.49 億元，季增 61.9%/年增 611.7%，其中 1) 平均售價(ASP)：季增超過 60%/年
增超過 500%， 受 惠 半 年 期/一年期等 LTA 重 新 議 價 及 季 度 合 約 價 調 整 、2) 位 元 出 貨 量(Bit
shipment)：季減中個位數百分比 /年減低個位數百分比，主因新廠產能尚未開出且庫存處於低水位；
毛利率達 85.7%，季增 6.2ppts/年增 67.2ppts，受惠 ASP 大幅成長；營業利益率 80%，季增
6.3ppts/年增 74ppts，費用方面提列當期及未來之員工薪酬增加；稅後獲利 876.27 億元，季增
74.6%/年增 5505.1%；EPS 25.40 元，整體 3Q26 獲利大致符合市場預期。

供不應求延續下，2027 年 DRAM 合約價仍維持高檔且獲利續創新高
本次法說會重點包括：1) 公司表示 DDR4 市場環境健康，DDR4 目前佔整體 DRAM 市場需求仍有
約 12~14%，雖需求逐漸縮小但對應大幅縮減的供給仍相當強勁，ASP 仍有望保持雙位數成長；2)
2026 年資本支出指引不超過新台幣 697 億元，2026~2029 年 5A 新廠資本支出 預算約新台幣
3,466 億元，屏東新廠長期資本支出規劃達 100 億美元，主要用於 AI 應用的 WoW 3D 晶圓封裝與

測試需求，預計 2H28 起設備裝機； 3) Server/AI 相關目前營收占比 >20%，公司指引將成長至
2029~2030 年的 40~50%，預期隨新產能上線持續提升。
展望 2026/2027 年，目前月產能推估約 65kwpm，而 5A 新廠具備約 50kwpm 的擴充空間，目前規
劃 2028 年將投產至 30kwpm，新增產能預計於 2H27 開始微幅貢獻，相關資本支出預算約新台幣
3,466 億元(含 EUV 設備)，長期而言公司亦預計於屏東科學園區投資設立 3D 晶圓封裝測試廠，導
入矽穿孔(TSV)、晶圓堆疊等 3DIC 先進技術；位元出貨量方面，2026 年在晶圓產出尚待新廠產能
開出、以生產效率提升與 16nm 製程微幅增加貢獻下，公司維持年增 high-10s % 的指引，2027 年
考量高基期且新廠產能於下半年才開始貢獻，預估 2H27 前位元出貨量維持平穩。
ASP 則受惠市場供需緊俏，1~3Q26 每季皆季增約 60~70%，4Q26 估亦將維持 15~20%的雙位數

季增幅；進入 2027 年，DDR4 供不應求態勢延續，但考量報價已處高檔、非 AI 下游終端漸難以負
荷，且記憶體原廠毛利率多已達 80%以上，預估 2027 年各季 ASP 持平至季增個位數百分比。
綜合上述，預估 2026/2027 年營收年增 525.5%/66.8%至 4,165.31 億元/6,946.17 億元；毛利率年
增 60ppts/2ppts 至 82.5%/84.5%，2026 年毛利率受惠 ASP 大幅年增已顯著提升至高水位，2027
年報價上行趨勢持續但逐漸持穩於高檔，折舊方面， 2026 年公司預估資本支出以不超過 新台幣
697 億元為上限，其中約 50%用於設備採購，2027 年隨 5A 新廠投產所需設備投資持續增加，資
本支出將大幅年增，故在 2026 年隨先前廠房設備折舊陸續到期使折舊成本相對低之後，2027 年折
舊金額將明顯增加，但在目前 ASP 與獲利皆維持高檔且加計新產出貢獻下，整體 2027 年毛利率受
折舊影響可控；營益率年增 68.8ppts/2.2ppts 至 76.7%/78.9%；稅後獲利年增 3861%/68.6%至

2,619.4 億元/4,416.29 億元；EPS 75.93/128.01 元。
除報價與產能拉升外，中長期可關注趨勢包括： 1) Server/AI 相關應用占比持續提升、 2) DDR5
16Gb 6400/7200Mbps 及以上貢獻增加、3) WoW 客製化高頻寬記憶體放量。

### Page 5

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：記憶體 2026 年 10 月 27 日 Nanya Tech 第5 頁，共 20頁  
產品結構分析 
受惠 DDR4 供需緊俏及寡佔優勢、Server/AI 占比續增，亦具備 DDR5 產品布局 
雖三大國際原廠 DDR4 EOL 以將產能轉向 HBM/DDR5，中國同業長鑫存儲(CXMT)亦將重心轉往
DDR5，但 DDR4 於消費性/網通/Server 等仍具龐大需求，在供給大幅縮減下，公司目前已為全球
第一大 DDR4 供應商，高度寡占且具備價格主導權，除 8Gb DDR4 以外，16Gb 亦於 2H26 開始貢
獻營收。公司目前 Server/AI 相關應用營收比重已超過 20%，且占比將隨新產能開出而提升，持續
受惠 Storage、BMC、NIC、Switch 應用等貢獻，相較國內同業有望減緩消費性疲弱影響。 
DDR4 為公司目前產業地位、獲利最有利的產品組合，但其亦具備 DDR5 技術能力與相關營收貢獻，
目前採 1B 製程的 16Gb DDR5 已量產出貨，傳輸速率現以 6400Mbps 為主並持續開發 7200Mbps
及以上規格。未來若終端客戶需求加速由 DDR4 轉向 DDR5，公司得以靈活調整產能配置以增加出
貨，持續拿下市場份額。除一般型 DRAM 以外，公司亦有 LPDDR4 產品出貨，LPDDR5 則有望於
2027 年導入量產。 
圖 8：目前營收仍以 DDR4 為主

圖 9：1B/1C 營收占比持續提升

資料來源：公司資料、元大投顧整理 資料來源：元大投顧預估 
WoW 客製化高頻寬記憶體為長期成長利基，布局 Edge AI 與外溢市場需求 
WoW(Wafer-on-Wafer)客製化高頻寬記憶體為有利公司中長期營運體質的重要成長動能。目前的標
準 HBM 為 IC 對 IC 堆疊，主要用於 Cloud AI 訓練等市場，然而 Edge 端裝置受限於功耗、成本與
物理體積等限制無法直接採用，同時三大國際原廠有限之 HBM 產能亦無法完全滿足整體 Cloud 端
市場需求，公司亦將受惠此外溢效果。其客製化產品採用 Wafer-on-Wafer Hybrid Bonding( 晶圓級
混合鍵合)技術，將 DRAM 與邏輯 Core Die 直接對接堆疊，能以顯著之成本與功耗優勢滿足客戶於
Edge 端的高頻寬需求。目前有多個客戶專案進行中，預估 2H27~2028 年可見較清晰的量產營收，
新廠產能規劃上亦保留彈性予 WoW 客製化 DRAM 專案，長線有望為公司提供較穩定的獲利支撐。 
圖 10：Wafer-on-wafer 堆疊技術示意圖

資料來源：Samsung、元大投顧整理

### Page 6

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：記憶體 2026 年 10 月 27 日 Nanya Tech 第6 頁，共 20頁  
財務分析 
整體財務結構於產業上行及下行循環期間皆維持穩健 
公司在 2020~2022 年 上 行 循 環 期 間 ， 流 動 比 率 與 速 動 比 率 分 別 高 達2020/2021/2022 年的
874%/678%/652%及 707%/599%/502%，後續下行期間雖降至 2023/2024 年的 480%/329%及
339%/217%，但整體仍位於健康水準，且負債比率在上行及下行期間均維持在低於 20%的低水準，
短長期償債能力無虞。2025 年以來隨著記憶體市況轉佳、報價顯著上漲，加上 1H26 完成 787.18
億元之私募案，流動比率與速動比率再回升至 2Q26 的 691%與 635%，負債比率則為 17%，預期
後續持續優化。 
經營能力方面，應收/應付帳款週轉天數落在 36~60 天/19~48 天的健康區間，且兩者差距在合理範
圍內。然而，存貨週轉天數受記憶體景氣循環影響，由上行循環期間 2020/2021 年的 130/97 天，
攀升至下行循環期間 2023/2024 年的 271/333 天，使整體現金轉換週期由 2021 年的 120 天拉長至
2024 年 337 天，反映景氣谷底期間庫存積壓對營運資金運轉效率帶來短期壓力。不過隨著 2025
年以來 DRAM 報價顯著上漲且公司庫存去化加速，2Q26 存貨週轉天數已降至 124 天，帶動現金轉
換週期縮短至 118 天，預期將持續改善。 
觀察現金流與資本支出動向，公司於 2021 年達到 328.47 億元的自由現金流高峰，隨後於
2022~2024 年下行期間，公司雖降低但仍投入 100 多億元的資本支出以推動製程技術組合轉往
10nm 級發展，自由現金流 2023/2024 年轉為淨流出 193.4/141.91 億元。儘管自由現金流短暫承壓，
公司在手現金仍穩健維持於 500 億元以上之充沛水準， 2025 年進入上行循環後營運顯著轉佳，
1H26 自由現金流已達 805.52 億元，加上引進 Kioxia、SanDisk、Solidigm 及 Cisco 之高達 787.18
億元的私募案挹注，使 2Q26 底在手現金大幅提升至 2,176.03 億元的歷史新高，且隨著營運成長
將持續增加，足以支應 5A 新廠未來四年高達 3,466 億元之資本支出規劃，整體財務結構穩健。

產業概況  
2027 年 DRAM 供需緊俏與報價上行持續，主要由 Server 需求驅動 
本中心預期 2027 年 DRAM 將持續供不應求，報價仍在上行循環，其中最主要驅動力為 Server 相
關 DRAM 訂單需求大幅成長且優於預期，Server 佔整體 DRAM 位元需求比重持續增加，預期自
2026 年的 54%成長至 2027 年的 63%，供需差更達 20%以上，高於整體 DRAM 的約 10%，主因：
1) Server 需求大幅成長，如本中心預估 2026/2027 年 ASIC 晶片量年增 91%/88%；2) Server 單位
DRAM Content 持續提升；3) AI Inference/Agentic AI 帶動 DRAM 儲存需求多樣化，如擴大導入
LPDDR(SOCAMM)於 Server 架構中，其中預估 2027 年 Nvidia VR200 機櫃對應 CPU 搭配的
SOCAMM2 位元需求成長高達 6 倍。

預期 2027 年 HBM 整體市場規模年增 243%，其中位元出貨量年增 55%、ASP 翻倍成長，主因：1) 
HBM 先前因年度議價機制無法反映 2H25 以來 DRAM 漲勢，近期已重新議定 2027 年度合約價；2) 
HBM4 放量出貨，其中 HBM4 佔整體 HBM 產值比重將達 46%。此外，雖 Rubin Ultra 、TPU v9 搭
配的 HBM4E 將由 12Hi 降至 8Hi，但主要為因應供給不足下為確保出貨顆數的應對措施，並非需求
下修。2027 年 HBM 市場份額預期 SK Hynix 約 40~45%、Samsung 約 35~40%、Micron 約
25~30%，SK Hynix 仍為 HBM 龍頭，但因 Samsung HBM4 進度優於同業、SK Hynix 持續調整
HBM4 效能，使 Samsung 市佔率具提升空間。

資料來源：元大投顧預估 資料來源：元大投顧預估 
以 BOM Cost 分布來看，DRAM/NAND 漲價對於 General Server 與 PC 價格之影響程度較高，如
DRAM/NAND 漲 10%，對於 AI Server/ General Server/PC/Smartphone 整機價格之影響分別為
1%/5.3%/3~3.6%/2~3%。 
而組裝廠更改記憶體規格之彈性來看，PC 最高、Server 其次、AI Server 接續、Smartphone 最低。
其中 PC 品牌廠因各地所需之規格不同且主機板可調整彈性相對手機高，加上每年都會針對原有的
零組件對供應商要求降價，因此可以最快調整其記憶體設計以因應記憶體價格變化，因此將較快出
現產品降規之情況；Server 部分以 Storage Server 所受之影響最大，尤其對於以中小企業客戶為
主之白牌業者其價格敏感度較高，不過對 CSP 來說對於 General Server/AI Server( 占比小)漲價之
接受度仍高；而 Smartphone 則只能在下一代產品發布前更改設計，多只能自行吸收或是對下游反
映售價，對於品牌廠毛利與終端需求影響較大。

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：記憶體 2026 年 10 月 27 日 Nanya Tech 第9 頁，共 20頁  
2027 年原廠 DRAM/NAND 新增產能有限，仍不足以因應強勁成長的需求 
雖 2027 年中部份新產能將上線，三大 DRAM 國際原廠年底投片月產能預估皆僅較 2026 年底增加
10~15%，長鑫存儲擴產幅度雖達 20~30%仍難以滿足中國國內需求 (目前中國 DRAM 自給率僅
30~40%)，即使搭配製程轉換以提升位元產出，仍 無法滿足本中心預估之全球 DRAM 位元需求量
30%以上的強勁成長，其中國際原廠擴產計畫如下： 
1) Samsung ：P4L 自 3Q25 起陸續投產，預計至 2027 年底月產能將達約 150kwpm，主要用於 1c 
DRAM 以因應強勁的 HBM4 需求，並持續推動現有廠房舊製程轉往 1c nm 以滿足 HBM 與通用型
1c DRAM 市場；此外，P5L Fab 1 已於 2025 年底復工、Fab 2 提前至 2026 年 7 月動工，產能目
標高於現有廠房約 50%以上。 
2) SK Hynix ：M15X 2026 下半年預計投產約 40kwpm，2027 年底月產能將達約 80kwpm，主要用
於 1b 並部分導入 1c DRAM ；公司亦表示 Y1 首個無塵室設備裝機將提前 3 個月至 2027 年 5 月，
以因應強勁的 AI 相關記憶體需求，預計至 2030 年上半年分階段開出共 360kwpm 的產能，Y2 則
預計 2027 年 7 月動工、2029 年 6 月投產，用於生產 HBM 及下世代 DRAM。 
3) Micron ：美國 ID1 預計 2027 年中產出第一批晶圓、ID2 則預計 2028 年底產出；向力積電添購
之銅鑼廠預計提前一季至 2027 年中出貨；新加坡廠區 HBM 封裝產能將於 2027 年上半年開始貢獻。 
4) 長鑫存儲：上海新廠 2027 年開始投產，主要針對 HBM3 等高端記憶體產品，亦新建後段封測廠
以填補中國在 TSV 及堆疊封裝的供應鏈空白，合肥 Phase 3 則預計以 LPDDR4X/5/5X 等產品為主，
預計 2028 年開始投產。 
圖 24：2027 年 DRAM CAPEX 將年增 56% 圖 25：2027 年 NAND CAPEX 將年增 27%

獲利預估與股票評價
正向看待南亞科後續營運，目標價 570 元
展望後續，在 ASP 維持高檔且新廠產能將於 2H27 開始逐步貢獻下，正向看待公司中長期營運成
長，預估 2026/2027 年營收年增 525.5%/66.8%至 4,165.31 億元/6,946.17 億元 ，毛利率年增
60ppts/2ppts 至 82.5%/84.5%，EPS 年增 3457.6%/68.6%至 75.93/128.01 元。
目前公司股價約在 1.8 倍 2027 年每股淨值(BVPS) ( 隱含本益比 3.2 倍)，低於國外同業平均的 2.0
倍，公司過去三年 PB 則落在 0.4~2.8 倍區間。雖目前循環處於 DRAM 報價仍維持高檔但預期後續
季增幅將收斂的階段，然而考量 1) 公司為 DDR4 第一大廠的產業地位且同時具備 DDR5 產品布局、
2) 產品組合將逐步擺脫消費性並持續往 Server/AI 提升(目前相關營收占比已超過 20%)、3) WoW

客製化產品有望帶來更穩定的獲利支撐，有助公司中長期營運更加穩健，以 2.5 倍 PB、2027 年
BVPS 230.7 元，推得目標價 570 元，給予買進評等。

公司簡介  
南亞科技成立於 1995 年，近年於全球 DRAM 市佔率約 1~2%，排名第五，公司目前以 DDR4 為主(佔比 60~70%)，亦具備
DDR5 產品，以應用別而言，PC & Server 約 25~30%、Mobile 約 10~15%、Consumer 約 60~70%。公司生產基地為新北
泰山 3A 廠，月產能 6~6.5 萬片。由於 DDR4/5 為長期主流市場，公司將陸續發展 1B/1C/1D 製程，且預計 2026~2029 年
投入新台幣 3,466 億元建置 5A 新廠產能，預估新產能將於 2H27~2028 年開出。

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：記憶體 2026 年 10 月 27 日 Nanya Tech 第17 頁，共 20頁

ESG 分析  
分別藉由「在 ESG 議題上的曝險」和「個別公司在 ESG 議題上的執行力」兩個面向來衡量公司於 ESG 上的表現。 
► ESG 總分： 南亞科整體的 ESG 風險評級屬於中等風險，於 Sustainalytics 資料庫所覆蓋的公司中排行在略為領先的位
置，但在半導體設計與製造的公司中排名領先同業。 
► 在 ESG 議題上的曝險： 南亞科的整體曝險屬於中等水準，與半導體設計與製造行業的平均水準相當。公司較需要改善
的 ESG 議題包含商業道德以及人力資源等。 
► 個別公司在 ESG 議題上的執行力：南亞科在針對重大 ESG 議題的管理水準和執行力屬較佳。ESG 報告體系完善，公
司已指定董事會成員負責監督 ESG 相關事宜。使用 ESG 目標評估高階主管績效；然而，這些目標並未在薪酬政策中明
確規定。儘管如此，公司的檢舉人保護計畫和環境政策均較為完善。

投資評等說明 
買進：根據本中心對該檔個股投資期間絕對或相對報酬率之預測，我們對該股持正面觀點。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股之發展前景、財務表現、利多題
材、評價資訊以及風險概況之分析。建議投資人於投資部位中增持該股。 
持有-超越同業：本中心認為根據目前股價，該檔個股基本面吸引力高於 同業。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股發展前景、財務表現、利多題材、評價資訊
以及風險概況之分析。 
持有-落後同業：本中心認為根據目前股價，該檔個股基本面吸引力低於 同業。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股發展前景、財務表現、利多題材、評價資訊
以及風險概況之分析。 
賣出：根據本中心對該檔個股投資期間絕對或相對報酬率之預測，我們對該股持負面觀點。此一觀點係基於本中心對該股之發展前景、財務表現、利多題
材、評價資訊以及風險概況之分析。建議投資人於投資部位中減持該股。 
評估中：本中心之預估、評等、目標價尚在評估中，但仍積極追蹤該個股。 
限制評等：為遵循相關法令規章及/或元大之政策，暫不給予評等及目標價。

### Page 3

Unimicron (3037.TW)
28 October 2026 Citi Research
3
Long-term strategies: Resources concentrated on ABF/ AI PCB — As we
highlighted, Unimicron has been converting BT capacity into ABF, which currently
causes some BT capacity to be idle during the transition period (negative impact will
likely last into 1H27, in our view). Thus, currently, we are less bullish on its BT GM
recovery in the near term vs other BT peers. However, we expect this conversion will

help the company to secure more ABF demand earlier considering customers
currently are still waiting for those ABF greenfield capacity expansion projects to
come in the next few years. For HDI/mSAP , Unimicron would only focus on AI
server/optical module products in the long term. To note that, the company is still
less aggressive in HDI capacity expansion given higher competition from Chinese
peers. Meanwhile, the company is also downsizing its traditional PCB capacity (i.e.,

for non-AI business) and just announced its plan to sell its PCB plant in Japan to
other players. We expect there will be more downsizing plans to come for traditional
PCB capacity.
GM expansion on track; reiterate Buy — We raise our 2026/27 /28E by 10%/5%/5%
to factor in robust GM expansion by ABF. We remain positive on its GM expansion by
ABF and also see potential upside coming from HDI and BT segments. We raise our
DCF-based TP to NT$1,550 (30x 2027E EPS) from NT$1,500 and reiterate our Buy

rating.

### Page 6

Unimicron (3037.TW)
28 October 2026 Citi Research
6
Unimicron
Company description
Established in 1990, Unimicron is the world's No.1 PCB maker and HDI board
maker. After the acquisition of PPT in late 2009, its revenue exposure to
substrate has surpassed 40%, with the rest almost equally shared by
conventional PCB and HDI. Unimicron has manufacturing sites in Taiwan and
China (Suzhou, Kunshan, Shenzhen). UMC is the largest shareholder, with a
~13% stake.

Investment strategy
We rate Unimicron shares a Buy. Our positive view is premised on: more
upside potential in the ABF segment thanks to product-mix improvements,
better pricing on more complex product upgrade, and capacity expansion.
Despite macro uncertainty, from a long-term perspective, we believe the
tightness in ABF is unlikely to ease without much new capacity ramped
aggressively in the upcoming years, and Unimicron should be the largest
beneficiary with its scale in ABF. Thanks to AI GPU/ASIC demand, we now see

strong growth in 2026/27E ABF consumption. We believe Unimicron could
see strong revenue growth again as it did in 2019–21 from both ABF new
capacity and a BT rebound.
Valuation
Our target price for Unimicron is NT$1,550. We use a 1-year forward-looking
DCF-based valuation (ie, end-2027) to reflect Unimicron's ability to generate
stable cash. With a risk-free rate of 5.0% (10-year government bond rate), a
market risk premium of 7%, and an equity beta of 1.43 (2-year weekly share

price), we calculate Unimicron's WACC as 14.6%. Our target price is equivalent
to 62x/30x 2026/27E EPS, which is higher than the +1std level of consensus
PE multiple of the past five years. However, as the market focuses on 2027
upcycle, we believe valuation on 2027E EPS is still in line with historical peak
valuations of 25–30x during the last upcycles.
Risks
Key downside risks that could mean Unimicron shares trade below our target
price include: 1) weaker-than-expected HDI business; 2) significant increase

in ABF supply in the industry; and 3) stronger-than-expected margin pressure
from depreciation due to higher capex investments.
Prepared for Kevin Lu

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk

Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment
rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered

stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating
and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also

suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price
chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in

exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the
expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility

or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:
Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside

call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is
moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day

period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be
different from and does not affect a stock’s fundamental equity rating, which reflects a longer-term total absolute return

our ratings distribution table for our Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside rating system. However, we reiterate that we do
not consider No View to be a recommendation. For all Catalyst Watch/STV Upside/Downside calls, risk exists that the
catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

3481 群創光電(INNOLUX)

壹、訪 談／法 說 內 容 
重點摘要： 
 2Q26 營收 637 億，毛利率 14.6% ，營利率 2.6% ，EPS 0.57
元。受惠於非顯示器比重持續提升 1H26 EPS 達 0.77 元。  
 公司今年處分三座廠房，累計獲利約為 200 億元，目前資產處
分已告一段落 ， 暫無進一步出售廠房計畫 ， 後續將配合FOPLP
投資調整既有廠區資源。  
 因上半年提前拉貨以及零組件漲價影響，消費型顯示器需求動
能相對保守；半導體事業部分， Chip-first 製程已進入量產，
每月出貨量約 4,000 萬片，而 RDL-first 與 TGV （玻璃基板）
製程部分，目前正與客戶緊密合作推進，預期玻璃基板封裝產
品將於 2028 年推出。

一、 2Q26 毛利率達 14.6% ，EPS 0.57 元 
群創 2Q26 營收 637 億，QoQ -4.6% ，YoY +13.1% ， 而獲利方面，
2Q26 營業毛利 92.7 億， QoQ -3.6% ，YoY +96.8% ，毛利率
14.6% ，QoQ +0.1ppts ，YoY +6.2ppts ，營業淨利 16.3 億，營利
率 2.6% ，2Q26 業外淨利 16.3 億，稅後淨利 46.5 億，單季稅後
EPS 0.57 元。而就上半年來看， 1H26 消費型顯示器佔整體營收
45% ，平均毛利率為 6%至 10% ；商用顯示器佔 13% ，平均毛利
率 16% 至 20%； 非顯示器領域佔42%， 平均毛利率11% 至 15% ，
受惠於商用、非顯示器比重提升， 1H26 EPS 達 0.77 元。

二、資產處分告一段落，後續聚焦 FOPLP 產能  
為充實營運資金、活化公司旗下資產，群創今年處分三座廠房， 3
月將南科模組廠以 8.8 億元售予南茂 (處分利益 6.59 億元 )，以及
將南科二廠以 63.25 億元售予矽品 (獲利約 58 億元 )。公司 4 月再
度宣布，將南科五廠房及附屬設施以總價 148.5 億元售予日月光
投控，預計處分利益高達 133 億元。累計今年群創認列的售廠總
獲利約為 197.59 億元。公司表示，目前資產處分已告一段落，暫
無進一步大規模出售廠房計畫 ， 後續將配合FOPLP 投資調整既有
廠區資源。

三. 消費性面板短期保守，持續朝高階封裝領域布局  
公司公告 9 月營收 199.95 億， MoM +6.9% ，YoY +0.7% ，3Q26
營收 575.8 億， QoQ -9.4% ，YoY -0.4% ，今年累計營收 1879.3
億， YoY +10.6% 。展望後市，在面板業務方面，受記憶體等零組
投資評等：區間操作 
目標價位：42.8~54.6 元 
     1.45x-1.85x27BVPS

件漲價及筆電客戶提前於上半年拉貨影響，對消費性顯示器短期
動能相對保守，惟 50 吋以下 TV、高階電競與 OLED 顯示器，以
及商用顯示器支撐營運表現。而半導體事業部分，公司表示，目
前 Chip-first 製程已進入量產，每月出貨量突破 4,000 萬片，預計
今年與 2027 年將維持雙位數年增率 ， 至於RDL-first 與 TGV 製程
部分，由於 TGV 玻璃基板具備優異的散熱效率、低翹曲以及高密
度互連優勢，將瞄準 AI 與 HPC 晶片需求，目前正與全球策略夥
伴及客戶緊密合作推進 ， 預期玻璃基板封裝產品將於2028 年前推
出。預 估2026 全年營收 2451 億元 ，YoY +8.1% ， 毛利率14.2% ，
營利率 1.9%， 稅後淨利805 億 ， 全年稅後EPS 1.01 元。預 估2027
全年營收 2659 億元，YoY +8.5% ，毛利率 14.3% ，營利率 3.1% ，
稅後淨利 892 億，全年稅後 EPS 1.12 元，每股淨值 29.51 元。

四. 投資建議為 42.8~54.6 元區間操作  
公司持續投入半導體 FOPLP 領域，聚焦轉型、資產活化題材，但
考量到 4Q26 至明年上半年面板事業展望保守 ， 半導體事業部分，
後續仍須持續觀察客戶認證、量產進度及營運貢獻狀況。目前股
價交易於 2027 BVPS 約 1.64 倍 PBR 水準，以公司過往 PBR 介
於 0.4x~2.5x 區間水準，給予區間操作投資建議，目標價為
42.8~54.6 元(1.45x~1.85 x 2027 BVPS) 。

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介  
群創光電 (INNOLUX) 為全球第三大面板廠，主要產品為大尺寸 (大
於 9 吋)與中小尺寸 (9 吋以下 )與延伸技術之薄膜電晶體液晶顯示
器(TFT-LCD) 相關產品，大尺寸產品應用於液晶電視、公共看板、
桌上型監視器、筆記型電腦與工業控制等；中小尺寸則為平板電
腦、可攜式影音播放器、車載、航空船舶、智能家居及手機、穿
戴裝置等應用，也可選擇性搭配各類型觸控面板；另針對特殊使
用需求，也提供應用於醫療、軍用、教育與電子紙應用之產品。
集團總部位於苗栗縣竹南鎮，台灣生產基地位於竹南、南科、台
南、高雄等地，另有海外生產基地則包含中國深圳、中國寧波、
中國南京、中國南海、荷蘭等地。 2025 年公司營收占比大宗消費
品(Commodity) 55% ；非大宗消費品 (Non-commodity) 16% ；非
顯示器領域群 (Non-display) 29% 。公司 NB 客戶為 DELL，液晶電
視客戶包括 Panasonic 、Sharp 、Sony、Toshiba 、Samsung 、TCL
等；手機及平板電腦客戶為 Samsung 、Amazon 、Apple 及華為；
車用面板客戶包括 BMW、福 特、General Motors 、 特斯拉等車廠。
國內主要競爭廠商為友達光電、瀚宇彩晶，國外廠商則包括

2. 群創連售三廠房，預估獲利近 200 億元

億元 )，以及將南科二廠以 63.25 億元售予矽品 (獲利約 58 億元 )
後，群 創 於15 日再度宣布 ， 將南科五廠房及附屬設施以總價148.5
億元售予日月光投控，預計處分利益高達 133 億元。累計今年以
來，預估公司可望認列售廠總獲利達 197.59 億元。

4 
二.產業趨勢概況  
1. AI 需求推動，FOPLP 與玻璃基板市場 2030 年將突破 80 億美元 
根據  Counterpoint Research 報告，受惠於 AI 與高效能運算
(HPC) 強勁需求 ， 全球扇出型面板級封裝(FOPLP) 與玻璃基板市場
規模，預計將從 2024 年的 6.5 億美元飆升至 2030 年的 81 億美
元以上，其中 AI 與 HPC 應用將佔 FOPLP 總營收的 45.6% 。相較
於傳統有機基板，玻璃基板具備高互連密度、尺寸穩定與抗翹曲
等優勢，能支援次世代小晶片 (Chiplet) 架構與大型 AI 處理器，吸
引台積電、 Intel 、三星電機、日月光及力成等大廠積極擴展產能
與研發。區域佈局上，預計 2030 年台灣、日本與中國大陸將囊括
全球 84.8% 的面板級封裝產能。儘管目前仍面臨面板尺寸標準化
與玻璃穿孔 (TGV) 製程等量產挑戰，但在產業鏈合作推動下，整體
市場前景依舊十分樂觀。

圖三、 2024-2030 年各地區 FOPLP 面板產能預估

資料來源： Counterpoint

2. 零組件成本壓力與需求衰退交織，10 月面板價格全面持平 
根據 TrendForce 預估，進入第 4 季後，受上游零組件成本上漲壓
力遽增及終端採購需求衰退雙重影響， 10 月 TV、MNT 與 NB 三
大面板價格預估將全面持平。 TV 面板部分，雖有原廠調控產能並
喊漲，但主要品牌第 4 季採購需求季減約 5%，短期難接受漲價；
而 MNT 面板需求持續減弱，惟成本壓力使面板廠無讓步空間，買
賣雙方對持平具共識，後續將視 TV 面板能否成功漲價而定；另
外， NB 面板則因前六大品牌第 4 季採購需求恐季減近 30% ，出
貨動能嚴重萎縮，在成本高漲且降價無助爭取訂單的情況下，雙
方亦達成維持價格穩定的共識。

叁、業 績 及 獲 利 預 估

二、資產處分告一段落，後續聚焦 FOPLP 產能  
為充實營運資金、活化公司旗下資產，群創今年處分三座廠房， 3
月將南科模組廠以 8.8 億元售予南茂 (處分利益 6.59 億元 )，以及
將南科二廠以 63.25 億元售予矽品 (獲利約 58 億元 )。公司 4 月再
度宣布，將南科五廠房及附屬設施以總價 148.5 億元售予日月光
投控，預計處分利益高達 133 億元。累計今年群創認列的售廠總
獲利約為 197.59 億元。公司表示，目前資產處分已告一段落，暫
無進一步大規模出售廠房計畫 ， 後續將配合FOPLP 投資調整既有
廠區資源。

三. 消費性面板短期保守，持續朝高階封裝領域布局  
公司公告 9 月營收 199.95 億， MoM +6.9% ，YoY +0.7% ，3Q26
營收 575.8 億， QoQ -9.4% ，YoY -0.4% ，今年累計營收 1879.3
億， YoY +10.6% 。展望後市，在面板業務方面，受記憶體等零組
件漲價及筆電客戶提前於上半年拉貨影響，對消費性顯示器短期
動能相對保守，惟 50 吋以下 TV、高階電競與 OLED 顯示器，以
及商用顯示器支撐營運表現。而半導體事業部分，公司表示，目
前 Chip-first 製程已進入量產，每月出貨量突破 4,000 萬片，預計
今年與 2027 年將維持雙位數年增率 ， 至於RDL-first 與 TGV 製程
部分，由於 TGV 玻璃基板具備優異的散熱效率、低翹曲以及高密
度互連優勢，將瞄準 AI 與 HPC 晶片需求，目前正與全球策略夥
伴及客戶緊密合作推進 ， 預期玻璃基板封裝產品將於2028 年前推
出。預 估2026 全年營收 2451 億元 ，YoY +8.1% ， 毛利率14.2% ，
營利率 1.9%， 稅後淨利805 億 ， 全年稅後EPS 1.01 元。預 估2027

Document 248: Australian Business Conditions and Confidence, September (2026-10-28)

### Page 1

#businessconditions #confidence #aud #australia
Australian Business Conditions and Confidence, September
By Luka Belobrajdic
Economist, Westpac
14:15 October 13 2026 Share
Confidence posts its sharpest fall since the start of the Middle East conflict as elevated fuel
prices and higher interest rates weigh on business sentiment, while conditions hold steady
at weak levels. Conditions unchanged at -1 / Confidence -4pts to -13.

### Page 2

The September NAB Business Survey was in the field from 23 to 30 September, capturing ongoing
global uncertainty, still-rising fuel costs and, at least in part, the RBA’s September rate hike. Business
confidence continued to deteriorate, recording its sharpest fall since March and the onset of the Middle
East conflict. The index declined 4pts to -13 and, while remaining above the lows recorded earlier this
year, is now at its weakest level since May. At 18pts below its long-run average, the result underscores

the deep pessimism that continues to weigh on businesses. This broadly mirrors the pessimism evident
in the Westpac-Melbourne Institute Consumer Sentiment Index, where elevated fuel prices and higher
interest rates have coalesced in a similarly weak assessment of the outlook.
The headline business conditions index held steady in negative territory at -1, albeit rising 0.25pts on
an unrounded basis. While encouraging that conditions have not deteriorated further, the index has

now remained at -1 (rounded) for two consecutive months, its weakest level since the height of the
COVID-19 pandemic in 2020. At 7pts below its long-run average, the result points to continued
pressure across the business sector.
Across the major sub-components, employment moved into negative territory, falling 3pts to -1 and
sitting 4pts below its long-run average. This marks the first outright negative reading since November
2020 and provides further evidence of the gradual easing in labour market conditions. Against the

backdrop of compressed margins, the result may also indicate firms are becoming increasingly
cautious around hiring decisions. By contrast, trading conditions rose 1pt to 4, although they remain
8pts below their long-run average. The relative resilience in trading conditions is broadly consistent with

### Page 3

Westpac DataX Card-Tracker data, which suggests nominal spending remained resilient, albeit
slowing, through the September quarter despite ongoing headwinds facing consumers. Profitability
recorded the strongest improvement across the major components, rising 3pts to -5, although the sub-
index remains deeply subdued at 10pts below its long-run average.
Capacity utilisation edged 0.2ppt lower to 82.3%. While the monthly decline provides a tentative signal

of easing capacity pressures, utilisation remains 0.9ppt above its long-run average and continues to
trend higher on a three-month average basis. Capital expenditure continued to recover from its mid-
year weakness, rising 3pts to 7. While still below the highs recorded in the first quarter, the sub-index is
now trending higher on a three-month average basis, pointing to some stabilisation in investment
intentions.
Cost growth was comparatively subdued in September, although purchase cost pressures remain

elevated. Purchase cost growth edged 0.1ppt higher to 2.4%qtr, sitting 1.1ppt above its long-run
average and continuing to weigh on margins. By contrast, labour cost growth eased 0.1ppt to 1.7%qtr,
although it remains 0.5ppt above its long-run average. Final product price growth was unchanged at
0.8%qtr, 0.2ppt above its long-run average. The widening gap between purchase cost and final product
price growth suggests firms continue to absorb a meaningful share of higher input costs, consistent

with profitability remaining deeply below average. Looking ahead, the October survey will provide an
early indication of how the card surcharging ban is affecting business costs and the extent to which
these are passed through to final prices.

### Page 4

Related articles
Among other indicators, forward orders declined 1pt to -1, pointing to continued caution around the
demand outlook. Inventories, by contrast, rose 4pts (rounded) to 7, some 5pts above their long-run
average. In combination with weak forward orders, the inventory build points to a degree of slack
emerging across the business sector.

COUNTRY RISK PROFILE
: STRONG ECONOMIC GROWTH AND LOW PUBLIC 
AND 
FOREIGN DEBT VS. LOW EXTERNAL 
LIQUIDITY AND 
A 
WEAK BANKING SECTOR

◼ 
 Strong factors support 
 Vietnam’s 
 overall country risk  
and economic prospects
 :  
i) 
a 
strong manufacturing export base that is moving up the value chain
 ; 
ii) 
s
olid comparative advantages that continue to attract large FDI inflows
 ; 
iii) 
c
urrent account surpluses and moderate foreign debt
 ; 
iv) 
a 
moderate government debt burden
 ; 
v) 
g
radually improving policy making
 ; 
vi) 
 s
trong medium
 -
 term economic growth prospects, assuming  
 that 
 the authorities will continue to implement structural reforms 
and strengthen productivity in the entire economy.

◼ 
 On the negative side,  
major negative factors continue to fuel some macro
 -
 financial risks:  
i) t
 he excessive domestic debt level of the economy (excluding the government) and fragilities in the banking sector 
 – 
this is a bad 
combination that fuels high credit risks and financial instability risks
 ; 
ii) 
 low forex reserves
 , which 
 have not much strengthened in recent years
 , 
 despite current account surpluses and notably due to 
resident capital flight. Forex reserves do not  
provide good  
protect
ion 
against shocks, while 
 Vietnam 
 is vulnerable to external shocks
 ;  
iii) 
 v
ery high exposure to physical climate risk.  
 C
oastal and river
 -
 delta regions, where population and economic activity are 
concentrated, 
 are 
 highly exposed to sea
 -
 level rise
 s 
and flooding
 .

◼ 
 Non transfer risk and currency risk
 : 
 V
ietnam  
 has enjoyed very 
 favourable  
 external account dynamics in recent years  
 until 2025
 , 
thanks to 
 large FDI inflows
 , 
recurrent 
 current
 -
 account surpluses  
and declining f
 oreign debt (
 which is moderate, at 
 less than  
30% of 
both GDP and exports). 
 This trend 
 has been  
interrupted 
 in 
 2026 
as 
current account surpluses 
 are 
 decreasing
 . Meanwhile
 , 
there is a 
persist
 ent 
 risk of 
 increasing 
 resident capital outflows 
 fuelling  
 some 
 VND 
 depreciation pressure  
 (
w
hereas  
 the potential for foreign 
capital outflows is limited
 )
. 
As 
the forex  
reserve cushion is weak
 , it would 
 not enable the 
 central bank 
 to 
defend the dong for a long 
time 
i
f there is  
severe currency and external liquidity pressure
 . 
However, so far this year, 
 the 
VND/USD spot rate and the reference 
rate 
 (Vietnam has a crawling band FX regime) 
 have depreciated only slightly
 , supported by strong exports and FDI inflows
 .

◼ 
 Sovereign risk
 : 
the government debt burden 
 is moderate and 
 will remain 
 so in the short term, even if the fiscal deficit is projected to 
deteriorate
 . The fiscal deficit could exceed 3% of GDP in 2026 due to support measures in response to the energy shock (estimated at 
less than 1% of GDP in H1 2026), but this will not endanger public
 -
 debt dynamics. Government debt should fall again in 2027 thanks 
to lower deficits and fast GDP growth. Debt was only 30% of GDP in 2025 (vs. 41% in 2019) and interest payments consumed less  
than 5% of fiscal revenue (vs. 7.2% in 2019). The government cover
 s its funding needs mostly in the local bond market, which is 
deepening gradually and not 
 highly 
 exposed to foreign investors. Government debt in foreign currency declined to about 25% of total 
debt in 2025 (vs. 38% in 2019); this is still substantial, but almost all this FC debt 
 is made up  
 of concessional loans from official 
creditors. On the negative front, high credit risks in the state corporate and bank sectors 
 are 
 very large contingent risks for the 
sovereign. Moreover, public investment needs related to climate change and infrastructure development will increase in the 
 medium 
term
.

◼ 
 Systemic credit risk
 : 
Domestic credit to the private sector is too large and still rising (141% of GDP in 2025 vs. 108% in 2019) while 
the banking sector remains poorly capitali
 s
ed. Credit risks and resource misallocation have long been exacerbated by opacity and 
supervision flaws, as well as the use of high credit growth targets to support economic growth (+15% in 2026). In 2022
 –
2023, banks 
faced severe funding stress and asset
 -
 quality deterioration resulting from the property and bond market crisis. The situation has 
recovered gradually since then. In the 
 short term
 , credit risks may increase amid higher energy costs and if  
there is  
a correction in 
the tech cycle. Some banks, especially small private ones, also remain exposed to VND funding risk (whereas banks do not rely  
much 
on 
 foreign
-
 currency 
 financing). Positively, reforms, including stricter capital
 -
 adequacy requirements, continue to be introduced 
gradually 
 in order 
 to improve banks’ creditworthiness (
 the 
 SBV plans to test the removal of credit quotas for the soundest banks 
from 2026).

#inflation #newzealand
First Impressions: NZ Consumers Price Index Q3 2026 and change
of OCR call
By Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
09:19 October 22 2026 Share
Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter and 3.7% y/y. Core inflation
pressures are elevated and market pricing is primed for a 25bp OCR hike next week, which
we now expect.
Consumers Price Index, September quarter 2026 – key stats

### Page 2

Headline inflation
Quarterly change: +0.7% (prev: +1.5%)
Westpac forecast: +0.7%
RBNZ: +0.8%
Market median: +0.8%, range +0.5% to +1.2%

Annual change: +3.7% (prev: +4.1%)
Westpac forecast: +3.7%, RBNZ: +3.9%, Market: +3.8%

Non-tradables
Quarterly change: +1.0% (prev: +0.6%)
Westpac forecast: +1.3%
Annual change: +3.3% (prev: +3.4%)

Tradables
Quarterly change: +0.2% (prev: +2.7%)
Westpac forecast: -0.2%
Annual change: +4.3% (prev: +4.9%)

Consumer prices rose 0.7% in the September quarter. That saw the annual inflation rate easing to
3.7%, down from 4.1% in the year to June. 
Today’s result was in line with our forecast, but lower than the RBNZ’s forecast for 3.9% annual
inflation. 
However, under the surface, the details were more concerning. Measure of core inflation have taken a
step higher, with several closely watched measures now at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s
target band. 
We suspect that today’s result will leave the market continuing to price a strong chance of another
OCR hike at next Wednesday’s policy review. In that light, we think the RBNZ will regard this as the
path of least resistance, and we now expect that they will deliver (previously we expected that they
would pause and assess, before hiking at the December MPS).

What contributed to inflation in the September quarter?
Underpinning September’s 0.7% rise in consumer prices were some big price movements in both
directions. 
The largest move in prices this quarter was actually a sharp fall in fuel costs. Fuel prices aren’t low by
any stretch of the imagination. However, average prices for both petrol and diesel over the September
quarter were lower than in the June quarter when prices rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle East

### Page 3

conflict. In terms of specifics, the average price for 91 unleaded was 6.5% lower in September than in
the June quarter, while diesel prices were close to 16% lower. Those moves took 0.4ppts off inflation
this quarter. 
While prices at the pump weren’t as high as they were in the June quarter, fuel costs are still painfully
high. And that’s been bleeding through into higher prices for other goods and services. Of note, the
September quarter saw large increases in passenger transport costs, with international airfares up 14%
and domestic airfares up nearly 8%. 
The September quarter also saw the annual increases in local council rates (up 6.2%) and charges for
services like water supply (up 7.5%). That continues the run of large increases seen in recent years.
Lastly, the past few months saw a 1.0% rise in food prices, largely as a result of the seasonal rise in
prices for fresh vegetables. 
On the downside, we’ve seen continued softness in housing rents, which are the largest component of
the CPI, accounting for 11.2% of the basket. With low population growth and ample supply, rents were
flat in the September quarter and have effectively been flat since the start of this year. In annual terms,
rental inflation is the lowest it’s been in more than two decades, with regions like Wellington seeing
outright falls.

Annual and core inflation 
The annual inflation rate dropped back to 3.7% in the September quarter, down from 4.1% in the year
to June and the highest level in two years.
Looking under the surface, prices in the domestically oriented non-tradables group rose 1.0% over the
quarter, less than expected. That saw annual non-tradables inflation nudging down to 3.3% (from 3.4%
in the year to June). 
Non-tradables inflation has continued to run at above-average rates over the past year. That’s despite
the softness in economic growth and the labour market. In large part, that’s due to the continued
sizeable increases in administered prices, such as electricity charges which rose 9.3% over the past
year. 
However, prices in other areas have also been firm. Non-tradables excluding housing and utilities costs
(which also omits local council rates) was 3.4% for the year. In seasonally adjusted terms, quarterly
non-tradables inflation has been tracking around 0.8% for most of the past year. It was noted that in
sectors like construction, higher materials costs are adding to prices. Feedback from business we've
heard indicates that those higher materials costs are likely related to high oil prices. 
On the imported front, lower fuel costs saw tradable prices rising by just 0.2% over the September
quarter (though above our forecast for a small fall). That saw annual tradables inflation dropping back
to 4.3%, down sharply from the 4.9% rise in the year to June. 
While fuel costs have been a key driver for tradables inflation, under the surface, other imported
inflation pressures are looking firm. Excluding food and fuel costs, tradables prices were up 2.7% over
the past year. That’s the highest level in three years. 
With big swings in fuel costs, and concern that this is spilling over into operating costs more generally,
a key focus for the RBNZ is what’s been happening to measures of core inflation (which smooth
through quarter-to-quarter swings in individual prices and instead track the underlying trend in

### Page 4

Related articles
inflation). 
On this front we did some see worrying signs. Several closely watched measures of core inflation have
picked up, and now sit at the top of (or slightly above) the RBNZ target band. In terms of specifics, we
saw: 
Reinforcing this picture of firm underlying inflation pressures, a growing number of goods have been
increasing in price. That could be an early indication that businesses, even those with a limited
exposure to fuel costs, are starting to factor in expectations of continued high inflation when setting
prices.

Outlook 
While today’s result was lower than the RBNZ assumed in their September MPS projections, the
firmness in core inflation will give the RBNZ pause for thought ahead of their policy review on 28
October. Furthermore, with a sharp rise in petrol prices in recent weeks, September’s easing in annual
inflation is likely to be just a temporary reprieve. We expect headline inflation to reaccelerate back over
4% before the end of this year, and it’s set to remain above the RBNZ’s current forecast for most of
2027. 
Market pricing and views have coalesced around an October hike which we now suspect the MPC will
see as the path of least resistance. Core inflation measures are now close to or above 3%, which will
add fuel to arguments from the MPC hawks. We will have more detail in our MPR Preview later today,
including on the implications for the shape of the tightening cycle moving forward.

CPI ex-fuel inflation: +3.0% yr (vs +2.9% previously)
CPI ex-fuel and food: 3.2% yr (vs +2.9% previously)
CPI ex-fuel, food and household energy: +2.9% yr (vs +2.5% previously)
30% trimmed mean: +3.0% (vs +2.6% previously)
Weighted median: +3.1% (vs +2.7% previously)

Document 354: Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Preview of 2026 New Zealand General Election (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• Polling points to a very close and unusually
fragmented election, with the outcome
likely to depend on the performance of the
Opportunity Party and whether any party
achieves “overhang” seats.
• While either Labour or National will lead the
next government, minor parties are likely to
have unprecedented representation in the next
government. This makes it harder to assess
the mix of policies that will emerge from

coalition negotiations.
• Compared to the status quo, a returned
National-led government with stronger
ACT and NZ First representation may be
more aggressive on some combination
of expenditure restraint, welfare and
immigration, resource development
and deregulation.
• A Labour-Green-Opportunity administration
would probably deliver more public-service
and social investment, a broader tax base,
stronger climate and labour regulation and
likely greater borrowing for infrastructure.

A centre-left government may find it hard to
resist pressure to lift public service salaries
and the minimum wage in response to fuel-
driven inflation.
• The fiscal starting point acts as a constraint:
the PREFU projects that OBEGALx will remain
in deficit until 2028/29 and achieving that
outcome will require exceptional spending
control in the current environment.
• Whichever government is elected will need
to make hard trade-offs, or raise additional

revenue or debt. The latter will be costly with
global investors requiring higher interest rates
to fund government (and other) debt.
• A key non-fiscal outcome of a change
in government would likely be the
implementation of the Labour Party’s
proposal to restore the RBNZ’s dual inflation-
employment mandate.
The election appears close, but the bigger
story is fragmentation.
New Zealand goes to the ballot box on 7 November
(although advance voting started on 26 October). The

campaign is taking place against a mixed economic
backdrop: activity has recovered somewhat, but
households remain sensitive to high living costs and a
weak housing market; the fiscal accounts have improved
relative to the Budget but still show deficits until 2028/29.
That combination gives the various parties room to
argue about priorities, but little room to avoid difficult
trade-offs. Additional spending – which may be difficult
to avoid in the current environment – will require either

additional revenue or a willingness to accept additional
debt issuance at a time when international investors are
seeking greater compensation to fund government debt
(and other).
The striking feature of this campaign is the reduced
dominance of the two major parties. Across the major
polls, neither traditional centre-left or centre-right
blocs can be confident of governing alone, and the
composition of the minor-party vote will determine both
who governs and how. Because the Opportunity Party

sits near the threshold, a movement of only a few tenths
of a percentage point could alter the allocation of several
seats. In addition, any “overhang” created by Te Pāti Māori
or the National Party winning more electorates than their
party vote entitlement could also move the majority line
above the usual 61 seats.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Preview of 2026 General Election.
27 Oct 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz

Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Together with uncertainty about voter turnout –
especially considering changes in voter registration rules
– the outcome of this General Election is difficult to call.
With major-party support unusually low and the minor
parties offering distinct, sometimes internally conflicting
programmes, the shape of the next government will be
determined not only at the ballot box on 7 November,
but at the negotiating table afterwards. Moreover, if the

results are especially tight, the outcome may not be clear
until sometime after the official results are declared on
27 November. And a hung parliament – where neither the
centre-left nor centre-right bloc can command a majority
– cannot be entirely ruled out either.
How New Zealand’s electoral system works.
New Zealand’s electoral system uses Mixed Member
Proportional representation (or MMP). Voters receive
two votes: an electorate vote for a local candidate and a

party vote for a political party. The electorate candidate
with the most votes wins the local seat. The party vote is
the main determinant of each qualifying party’s overall
representation in Parliament.
Parliament normally has 120 seats. For the 2026
election there are 71 electorates, comprising 64 general
electorates and seven Māori electorates, with the
remaining seats ordinarily filled from party lists. A party
qualifies for proportional representation by receiving

at least 5% of the nationwide party vote or winning an
electorate seat. List MPs are then added to electorate
MPs so that each qualifying party’s total representation
broadly matches its party-vote share.
An overhang arises when a party wins more electorate
seats than its party vote would otherwise entitle it to.
The party keeps those seats and Parliament expands,
which can raise the number required for a majority.
Government formation is separate from the allocation of

seats: a grouping must demonstrate that it can command
confidence in the House and pass supply. That can be
achieved through a formal coalition, a confidence-and-
supply agreement, or other support arrangements. The
largest party is not automatically guaranteed office and
minority governments are possible (e.g. although the
National Party secured more than 44% of the vote in the
2017 election, a minority Labour Party-NZ First coalition
was formed, supported by a confidence-and-supply

arrangement with the Green Party).
The overhang risk is unusually important in 2026 because
current polling leaves both blocs close to, but often
short of, a majority. In addition, the fragmentation of
the electorate increases the risk that one of the major
parties wins more electorates than implied by its party-
vote entitlement. This is in addition to the likelihood that
Te Pāti Māori will achieve an overhang given that it is
running an “electorate vote only” campaign and so asking

its supporters to cast their party votes for other left-
leaning parties. Each additional overhang seat expands
Parliament and so lifts the threshold needed to command
a majority.
The direction of the effect depends on which party
creates the overhang. Extra Te Pāti Māori electorate seats
would add MPs aligned more naturally with a Labour-
Green administration and could make Te Pāti Māori
support or abstention more consequential. A National
overhang would add seats on the right and could improve

the arithmetic for a National-ACT-NZ First government.
Under the latest RNZ-Reid Research projection, for
example, an overhang would arise if National won more
than 32 electorates or Te Pāti Māori won more than two.
This makes the party vote strategically important and
explains the focus on Opportunity’s threshold position.
A vote share just below 5% would produce no list seats
unless the party won an electorate (which based on
limited polling presently seems unlikely). A result just

above 5% would give it a parliamentary caucus large
enough, on current polling, to become the “kingmaker”
and decide which side can form a government. While
the Opportunity Party says that it could work with either
a centre-left or centre-right government, as detailed in
the appendix its broad policy platform might imply that it
more naturally aligns with the centre-left.
What the polls imply.
Kiwipoll’s six-poll tracker, last updated on 20 October,

put Labour on 27.9% and National on 28.7% - a gap
of just 0.8 percentage points. More strikingly, the two
major parties together attracted only 56.6%. The central
message across all polls is therefore fragmentation:
neither major party is close to governing alone, and almost
half of the represented vote is distributed among parties
that would shape the terms of any governing arrangement.
On that average, the balance between the established
blocs is also extremely narrow. The model associated

with the tracker assigned 56 seats to Labour, the
Greens and Te Pāti Māori, and 61 to National, ACT and
New Zealand First. Neither side reached a majority in a
presumed 124-seat parliament. Opportunity’s average
support of 6.2% translated into seven seats, enough to
take either bloc to the 63-seat threshold needed for a
majority. The average therefore implies that the identity
of the largest party may matter less than whether
Opportunity remains above 5% and which grouping it is

prepared to support.
This is not simply a close two-bloc contest. The polling
average also suggests that the minor parties would enter
negotiations with unusually large leverage. The Greens,
ACT and New Zealand First would consequently have
more scope than usual to influence tax, expenditure,
regulation, immigration and resource policy, or the
allocation of Cabinet positions, making it harder to
determine the likely form of a final coalition agreement.

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
That said, the leverage that these parties can apply on
their major party coalition partner is limited. The Green
Party cannot credibly threaten that it would form an
alternative coalition with the National Party. And neither
could ACT or NZ First credibly threaten to form an
alternative coalition with the Labour Party (while NZ First
has worked with the Labour Party in the past, it has ruled
out doing so on this occasion). Moreover, it is possible

that minor parties would be punished by the electorate
if a failure to form a government necessitated a second
general election, especially if the minor parties were
perceived to have made unreasonable demands or if the
electorate simply refocused on establishing a workable
parliamentary majority.
The Opportunity Party is the pivotal uncertainty because
its average position is above the threshold, but not
far above it. A result modestly below 5%, without an

electorate victory, would remove its votes from the
seat allocation and substantially change the arithmetic,
favouring the centre-right bloc. A result around its recent
6% polling average (or higher) would make the party
central to government formation.
Formally, National has ruled out working with
Opportunity, due to its objection to the latter’s tax policy.
For its part, Opportunity has said that it would talk first to
the party with the most seats, which on present polling

could be either Labour or National. However, as noted
previously, given Opportunity’s policy orientation, it
seems more likely that it would ultimately join a centre-
left coalition than a centre-right coalition.
To summarise, the bloc contest is close, and the route
to a majority depends on threshold and electorate
outcomes that remain uncertain. With that in mind, we
think that the following two scenarios are the most likely
outcomes of the election.

Scenario one: a returned centre-right
government, with greater minor party
representation.
The first scenario is that the existing coalition is returned,
albeit with the minor parties gaining strength and thus
additional leeway to pursue their policy agendas. We
assume that the coalition is not dependent on the
Opportunity Party to govern, which would be more likely
to occur if the latter fell marginally short of the party
vote 5% threshold needed to enter parliament in the

first place.
A renewed National-led government would begin with
considerable continuity. National’s fiscal plan retains
the current coalition’s focus on restoring surplus,
reducing debt as a share of GDP , keeping new spending
constrained and making greater use of planning reform
and private capital. It projects an OBEGALx surplus of
$4 billion in 2028/29, with net core Crown debt peaking
at 43.9% of GDP in 2027/28 and falling to 39.5% by
2030/31. National has ruled out new tax cuts during the

next term, saying tax relief should wait until the books
are back in surplus and surpluses are growing. But a
coalition in which ACT and New Zealand First held a
larger share of seats would be different from the current
more National-dominated administration. Unless its vote
share is particularly low, National would almost certainly
still provide the Prime Minister (sharing the role with NZ
First and ACT in the context of a three-year electoral
term seems quite unlikely – the latter will likely share the

Deputy role as they have in the current term). The three
parties share a common fiscal framework at the macro
level. However, National would have less ability to defer or
dilute its partners’ priorities.
Fiscal policy and the size of government.
ACT would press for a faster and deeper reduction in
the state’s footprint. Its fiscal plan proposes cutting
$9.5bn across the forecast horizon from spending outside
welfare and protected frontline services, reducing future

operating allowances by $400m a year to $2.0bn, and
moving non-welfare spending back towards 2017 real per-
capita levels. ACT says this would lower debt by $12.4bn
by 2031 relative to current forecasts. It also proposes
consolidating government into 19 departments and
18 ministers.
On its own, that agenda would pull a renewed
government beyond National’s more incremental
expenditure control. Coalition negotiations would likely
turn on the pace of implementation and the definition

of protected frontline services. The economic effect
would depend on whether savings represented genuine
efficiency gains or reductions in service capacity and
transfers. In the near term, a faster consolidation could
subtract from demand, while lower debt issuance
and a smaller structural deficit would strengthen the
fiscal buffer.
However, New Zealand First would push in a different
direction. Its programme combines selective tax relief
with active government intervention, regional grants

and strategic ownership. The tension within the coalition
would therefore not simply be between ‘more’ and ‘less’
government. ACT would seek a smaller and more market-
oriented state; New Zealand First would accept a larger
role where it supported national ownership, regions,
families or strategic industries. New Zealand First’s
proposal to reduce the corporate tax rate for SME’s to
20% (from 28%) may well be accepted by its coalition
partners during coalition negotiations – possibly phased

in over time – and would certainly be much cheaper
than the fiscal cost of some other policies promoted
by the party (notably compensating employees for
higher compulsory Kiwisaver contributions and the
nationalisation of the Bank of New Zealand).

### Page 4

4
Westpac New Zealand Economics
Tax, welfare and retirement.
ACT’s longer-run objective is a lower and flatter tax
system. Its immediate plan removes tax on qualifying
KiwiSaver investment earnings, ends the government
KiwiSaver contribution and returns net Emissions Trading
Scheme auction revenue to households through a
carbon-tax refund. Broader tax relief is deferred until
the books are balanced. ACT would raise the NZ Super
eligibility age by three months a year to 67 by 2035, while

allowing compassionate early access for people with long
careers in physically demanding work. It would also pause
contributions to the NZ Super Fund.
On welfare, ACT proposes tighter medical certification
and reassessment for disability-related benefits, a five-
year stand-down before migrants can access welfare,
and lower spending outside protected services. NZ
First would add its own eligibility restrictions, including
limiting NZ Super to citizens from 2029, while proposing

compulsory KiwiSaver enrolment from birth and a Crown
contribution. These policies would create difficult design
questions around transitional arrangements, hardship
and administration, but the broad direction would be
towards tighter eligibility and stronger conditionality.
Regulation, resources and labour markets.
A stronger ACT-NZ First presence would reinforce
deregulation in planning, employment and resource
development. ACT would seek property-rights-based

planning law, easier dismissal, a three-year minimum
wage freeze, a lower training wage for workers under
20 and removal of Te Mana o te Wai from consenting.
It would repeal the Zero Carbon Act. National’s own
RMA replacement and fast-track agenda would provide
substantial common ground.
NZ First would push more directly for regional and
strategic development: a special economic zone at
Marsden Point, offshore oil and gas surveying, expanded
mineral development, and the return of 50% of mining

royalties to producing regions. It also proposes breaking
up the power gentailers and has advocated bringing a
major bank back into New Zealand public ownership.
Both the National Party and NZ First have policies that
aim to force additional competition in the supermarket
sector, but these are opposed by the ACT Party.
Nonetheless, the result could be a government that is
simultaneously more deregulatory in consenting and
more interventionist in ownership and competition policy.

Immigration would also tighten. ACT proposes annually
reviewed work-visa categories, a Rural Workforce Visa,
stronger overstayer enforcement and physical-presence
requirements for permanent residents. NZ First proposes
replacing the Accredited Employer Work Visa with a
shortage-led Skilled Work Visa and holding a referendum
on a 20,000 annual cap on resident visas. National’s more
skills-focused approach would be under pressure from
both partners to place greater weight on enforcement,

settlement obligations and lower aggregate inflows.
Scenario two: Labour and the Greens,
supported by Opportunity.
The second main scenario that we view as likely is a
Labour Party-led coalition with the Green Party, with the
Opportunity Party supporting either inside the coalition
or via a confidence-and-supply arrangement. Depending
on precise outcome of the election, a confidence-and-
supply arrangement may also be required with Te Pāti
Māori to secure the necessary parliamentary majority.

A Labour-led alternative would represent a clearer break
with the current policy direction, but the size of that break
would depend on the success of the minor partners in
embedding their policies in the coalition agreement.
For its part, the Labour Party would aim to restore and
expand selected public services while remaining inside
its published fiscal framework. Its plan retains a $2.4
billion operating allowance for each Budget, targets an
OBEGAL surplus in 2028/29 and seeks to reduce net

debt below 20% of GDP over time. It would introduce
a 28% capital gains tax on post-1 July 2027 gains from
investment residential and commercial property, with
major exemptions, and use the revenue to help fund
health initiatives.
Labour’s central commitments include free prescriptions,
three free doctors’ visits, additional screening and
maternity services, 6,000 additional state houses,
public-transport fare caps, expanded apprenticeships, a
care-worker pay increase and partial reversal of planned

public-service cuts.
It would also restore the Reserve Bank’s dual price-
stability and maximum-sustainable-employment
mandate. As we have discussed in detail in a previous
note, in the current environment – dominated by supply
shocks – the return of the dual mandate could mean that
the RBNZ is prepared to accommodate a slower return of
inflation to target to reduce the impact on employment.
The Greens would shift the centre of gravity.
A strong representation of Green Party ministers in

the Cabinet would seek to move a coalition further on
redistribution, public ownership, housing and climate
policy. The party’s tax programme includes a 2.5% wealth
tax on net assets above $10 million excluding the family
home, a capital-acquisitions tax above $1 million with
exemptions, a 45% top personal rate, a higher company
tax rate for the largest firms, and a levy on large banks.
The stated objective is to raise about $5.1 billion net and
shift the tax burden from wages towards wealth and

large corporations.

### Page 5

5
Westpac New Zealand Economics
These taxes are needed to finance the Green Party’s
extensive promises on spending (see below). Formally,
the Labour Party has explicitly ruled out supporting the
Green Party’s tax policies to secure a Labour Party-led
coalition. Whether that pre-election commitment would
be reversed after the election is a matter of conjecture.
On spending and ownership, the Green Party proposes
free dental care and public transport, stronger income

support, expanded school lunches, more public housing,
a publicly owned renewable-energy entity and the
acquisition of supermarket stores to establish KiwiMart.
A centre-left government, especially with strong Green
Party representation, may find it harder to resist pressure
to lift public service salaries and the minimum wage
in response to fuel-driven inflation. Relative to PREFU
forecasts, this would imply additional expenditure that
would need to be met with either additional sources of

revenue or additional debt issuance.
Labour would resist parts of this agenda on cost,
implementation risk and fiscal credibility. The most likely
coalition bargain would therefore be a narrower set of
high-priority Green Party reforms, financed by some
combination of new revenue, reprioritisation and the
available Budget allowances.
The regulatory direction would be unambiguously
stronger. Labour and the Greens overlap on restoring
pay equity (which was not explicitly budgeted for in

Labour’s fiscal plan), strengthening worker protections,
improving rental standards, expanding public housing
and using government more actively in markets with
weak competition. Climate, freshwater and biodiversity
rules would tighten relative to the current government,
and mining on conservation land would face much
greater barriers.
Opportunity’s role, potentially outside Cabinet.
Opportunity could support a Labour-Green government,
either directly joining the coalition or through a confidence-

and-supply agreement. While the former would give
Opportunity seats at the Cabinet table, the latter
arrangement would let the party retain a distinct identity,
trading reliable support on confidence and budgets for a
defined policy programme. It may also provide more scope
for the party to “learn the ropes” with less public scrutiny,
given that none of its prospective MPs have ever been in
Parliament, let alone held Cabinet positions.
Regardless of what form the arrangement takes,

Opportunity’s landmark policies are neither a simple
extension of Labour nor of the Greens. Opportunity
proposes a “tax reset”, including a land value tax, with a
lower rate for rural land, alongside personal income tax
rates of 28%, 34% and 39%. The revenue raised would be
used to fund a “Citizen’s Income” payment to almost all
people, which would also replace several current social
welfare benefits. That said, Opportunity has conceded
that the land tax and Citizen’s Income are unlikely to be

implemented in the next Parliament given that it will
probably be the smallest of the coalition partners.
In negotiations, Opportunity would press for the
inclusion of its five bottom lines. These are (1) giving
the Commerce Commission more teeth and greater
independence to tackle excessive market power in
supermarkets and other uncompetitive sectors, (2)
regulating lobbying and capping political donations,
(3) developing a 10-year plan to "fix the health system"

alongside health professionals, with clear commitments
on funding, workforce and infrastructure, (4) phasing
out bottom trawling, working with industry, iwi and
coastal communities to fairly manage the transition,
(5) recommitting to Paris Agreement emissions
reduction obligations and protecting the Climate Change
Commission's independence. Opportunity’s other policies
include allowing net debt to exceed 50% of GDP if the
additional borrowing finances productive infrastructure.

It also proposes compulsory KiwiSaver contributions
rising to 6% for employees and employers (a policy it
shares with National), free public transport, stronger
competition enforcement, higher R&D spending and a
ten-year cross-party education plan. In a confidence
and supply arrangement Opportunity could reserve the
right to oppose other legislation it disagrees with, but
the arrangement would need sufficiently clear fiscal
guardrails to avoid repeated uncertainty over Budgets

and concerns about the stability of the Government.
The economic stakes.
The two scenarios imply meaningfully different policy
mixes, but neither government would operate without
constraints. The current fiscal forecasts already assume
a return to surplus and a declining debt ratio later in the
forecast period. Delivery depends on tight expenditure
control, revenue growth and an economic outlook
exposed to oil prices, global interest rates and domestic

productivity. Coalition agreements that materially add
to operating spending or reduce revenue would need
offsetting measures if the party heading the coalition
wished to preserve its stated fiscal track.
For monetary policy, the direct institutional contrast is
Labour’s proposal to restore the Reserve Bank’s dual
mandate. In practice, the larger near-term influence may
come through fiscal demand, labour market regulation,
migration and energy policy. A faster fiscal consolidation

on the right would tend to reduce demand pressure, while
a larger operating spending programme on the left would
increase demand pressure. Infrastructure investment
is somewhat different: if well selected and delivered, it
can lift productive capacity over the medium term, but
it can also add to near-term capacity pressure before
supply benefits arrive. And given the current interest rate
environment, the hurdle that must be crossed to justify
raising debt is higher than it has been for years.

### Page 3

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank
Research Institute
Diplomacy appears firmly back in play. The past month saw continued fighting and renewed diplomacy.
Reporting suggests that the US is prepared to extend troop deployments in the Middle East to 2027. Iran
targeted both US forces in the region and some tankers moving through the Strait, but has been unable to
prevent a significant rebound in crude flows through Hormuz. On 22 SEP, talks were mediated by Qatar,

Pakistan and Egypt, and on 24 SEP, Iran presented the US with a seven-day roadmap to a ceasefire leading
to a phased reopening of the Strait of Hormuz and a return to nuclear negotiations, conditional on a US
lifting of its blockade of Iran, sanctions relief and broader cessation of hostilities. The Trump
Administration rejected the proposal, but negotiations continue.
To watch will be whether October is the month the war transitions into a negotiated settlement, which

may hinge on whether Iran is willing to make some concessions on sanctions relief and the nuclear
question, and whether Trump is keen to demonstrate that the war has produced a political and economic
result in the final weeks before the 3 NOV midterm elections. It is plausible that the current diplomatic
channel could produce a temporary de-escalation mechanism as long as there is pathway around the
broader nuclear issue.
Both sides will reconvene for trade talks in Beijing on 8-9 October, which the EU views as a deadline for

“tangible results”. Brussels remains focused on its rising trade deficit with China – which has reached a
record €1 billion per day – and the impact of Chinese goods on European industry. The bloc has vowed to
take measures if talks fail.
While both sides could achieve progress in talks, a lasting breakthrough appears improbable, setting the
stage for more trade tensions into 2027. New EU trade investigations, curbs on Chinese imports, and EU-
level instruments intended to address alleged Chinese overcapacity, are possible outcomes, to which

Beijing has stated it would respond.
China-EU trade talks could also impact China-US relations. Should talks with Brussels fall short of averting
increased European trade pressure, we assess that Beijing may further prioritise near-term US stability. See
Rules of Engagement: Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties for more.

### Page 4

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank
Research Institute
Latvia will elect all 100 members of the 15th Saeima. Latvia, which borders Russia and Belarus, has been
among the most vocal supporters of Ukraine. The next government will set the country’s course on
defence spending, Ukraine aid, sanctions enforcement and border security. The field is highly fragmented
with the governing parties lacking a majority in the polls. The fragmentation matters as a weakened and

multi-party scramble could mean a slower, more unstable coalition.
Brazil’s first electoral round is on 4 OCT, with a runoff on 25 OCT. This is a critical election as it will
decide whether Latin America’s biggest economy continues President Lula’s state-led social spending or
pivots to a more fiscally-orthodox agenda under Bolsonaro. Polls have gone from a Lula lead in the spring
to a statistical dead heat between Lula and Bolsonaro. A Brazilian Supreme Court crisis – which has

implicated Supreme Court Justices and politicians on both sides – is weighing on the race. Congressional
and gubernatorial races will also impact the governability of the country.
This election will also shape whether the country hedges between Washington and Beijing under Lula or
tilts toward closer US trade and security alignment under Bolsonaro with potential implications for
tariffs, BRICS+ cohesion, and Latin America’s broader positioning between the two powers.

The meetings will take place in Bangkok, bringing finance ministers, central bankers, policy-makers and
business leaders together. The agenda is expected to centre on global growth, financial stability, debt,
trade and investment, development finance and the impact of AI on jobs and productivity, with particular
attention given to mobilizing private capital. The key watchpoint will be the outlook for global growth
and inflation, debt vulnerabilities, the future of the international financial architecture and how policy-

makers respond to increasing fragmentation in trade and capital flows.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in October 2026

### Page 5

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank
Research Institute
Leaders are expected to focus heavily on Ukraine, European security and defence, competitiveness and
the EU’s economic position in a more contested global environment. They will also take forward
negotiations on the 2028-2034 EU long-term budget. They set themselves a soft deadline in June to get
the budget broadly locked down because missing it pushes ratification into 2027 and risks a funding gap

for beneficiaries from JAN 2028. To watch will be a genuine breakthrough on the multiannual framework
or more likely what “substantial” progress may look like. Ukraine funding is the other pressure point. This
meeting will be important for understanding the direction of European defence spending and industrial
policy, the next phase of budget negotiations and the EU response to growing external economic
pressure and strategic dependencies.
The Canadian province of Alberta will hold a referendum, which includes a question about the province’s

future within Canada. This is not an independence vote itself but asking voters to choose between
remaining a Canadian province and asking the Alberta government to begin the constitutional process
required for a future binding referendum on separation. Current polling suggests “remain” is comfortably
ahead. The significance is around potential renewed uncertainty around Canada’s federal cohesion,
energy policy, fiscal transfers and Alberta's relationship with Ottawa and Washington. Even a modest

agreement to begin a process towards secession would likely keep alive equalization grievances as a
structural fault line in Canadian federalism feeding into political risk.
.
The event’s theme focuses on “Advancing Alliances in a New Era.” It will tackle how governments and
businesses respond to a world where geopolitics, economic security, supply chains and technology are
increasingly intertwined. There is likely to be a strong focus on the future of the transatlantic

relationship, including how Europe adapts to a more demanding security environment. There will also be
discussions of tariffs, supply chain resilience, critical capabilities and the weaponization of economic
interdependence. It will align with Chancellor Merz’s efforts to ensure Europe actively shapes outcomes
around strategic autonomy and reducing China exposure in manufacturing.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in October 2026

### Page 6

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank

The second summit will take place in Saudi Arabi, bringing EU and Gulf leaders together. The agenda is
expected to focus on regional security and the Middle East, energy and economic security, trade and
investment, infrastructure, technology and broader strategic cooperation. The security dimension will be
particularly salient given the current crisis in the region. Economically, the relationship is substantial: EU-

GCC trade reached €165.6bn in 2025.
To watch will be movement on the long-stalled EU-GCC free trade talks and visa-waiver requests from
Qatar and Saudi Arabia in addition to the extent of the focus on Gaza reconstruction, Iran and regional
security get relative to the economic agenda.
GCC states have consistently used these summits to press for clearer EU positioning on Middle East
conflicts in exchange for economic cooperation. The summit comes amid at a critical juncture where

Europe is recalibrating its external partnerships.
The Central Committee of the Communist Party of China will hold its Fifth Plenary Session in Beijing.
This will be one of the most important events on the country’s political calendar ahead of the 21st Party
Congress in late 2027.
The Plenum will focus on Party governance, and investors will scrutinise signals regarding leadership
changes and personnel reshuffles, institutional reforms, and political priorities for the next Congress.

While fifth plenums historically focus on economic planning, the development guidelines for the 15th
Five-Year Plan (2026–2030) were already adopted during the Fourth Plenum in OCT 2025, allowing the
upcoming session to focus on personnel and governance matters.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in October 2026

### Page 7

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics | October 2026
Deutsche Bank

Elections will be held for the 26th Knesset, the first since the October 7th attacks and wars in Gaza,
Lebanon and Iran. This is the first Knesset since 1988 to actually complete a full four-year term without
early dissolution. Benjamim Netanyahu remains the dominant single figure but faces a fragmented field:
Naftali Bennett’s new “Together” party is polling as the main rival bloc, alongside Gadi Eisenkot’s Yashar,

Benny Gantz’s Blue and White, and the further right Ben Gvir and Smotrich factions.
Israel’s proportional system means the election is effectively about coalition formation. Key faultlines to
watch: the ultra-Orthodox draft-exemption fight, how the war’s legacy and hostage/reconstruction issues
in Gaza factor into campaign messaging, and whether a Netanyahu-led bloc can reassemble a working
majority or whether the Bennett/Eisenkot-style “anyone but Bibi” coalitions gain enough traction. The

outcome will shape Israel’s posture on critical regional issues, including Gaza reconstruction, Iran policy
and West Bank settlement activity.
This summit is shaping up as a particularly important juncture against a more ambitious push to redefine
the relationship. During Mark Carney’s SEP visit to Strasbourg, he called for a comprehensive strategic
alliance with Europe. European Commission President von der Leyen went further, proposing that Canada

become the EU’s first “associate member”.
The agenda is expected to centre on trade and economic security, defense, critical minerals, energy,
digital cooperation and supply-chain resilience, building on the EU-Canada security and Defense
Partnership in the EU’s SAFE defense initiative. The summit will be watched for concrete progress on a
digital trade agreement and broader economic cooperation.

The foreign ministers’ meeting comes ahead of the December Leaders’ Summit in Miami under the US’
chairmanship of the G20 this year. Trump’s G20 year has organized around four expert tracks – trade,
innovation, energy and deregulation. The session is framed around economic prosperity, energy security
and technological innovation, a much more transactional focus than previous foreign ministerials, which

typically focused on geopolitical crisis management.
It will be a test of whether the G20 can sustain cooperation amid intensifying geopolitical fragmentation.
In addition, Russia has confirmed its intent to actively participate in the meeting. This would further
normalize Russia’s multilateral engagement. For Europe and other partners, a key question will be the
extent to which the G20 can be a forum for broader international cooperation.

Document 421: “It’s the economy, stupid.” K字化が揺らす米国中間選挙

Source PDF: all report pdf/“It’s the economy, stupid.” K字化が揺らす米国中間選挙.pdf
Report source: DIR

### Page 1

米国

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番 1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー 
このレポートは投資勧誘を意図して提供するものではありません。このレポートの掲載情報は信頼できると考えられる情報源から作成しておりますが、その正確性、完全
